[00:22:25] harder to secure employment, housing,
[00:22:28] and their lifestyle becomes unstable.
[00:22:30] And so for people who are already struggling, this can be another barrier that they
[00:22:33] carry for years.

[00:22:42] offenses under this amendment
[00:22:45] carry a maximum penalty of three months imprisonment.
[00:22:48] And we know that once someone enters the criminal justice system, the consequences
[00:22:51] can extend far beyond the statutory maximum penalty.
[00:22:55] So for example, those who we see regularly before the courts with long conviction
[00:22:59] histories and with difficulties with being granted bail
[00:23:03] are at risk of having remands in custody longer than the statutory maximum that
[00:23:07] they'd receive for offenses under the act.
[00:23:10] So that process itself can become a punishment, and it all stems from
[00:23:14] legislation that's directed at people whose underlying issue is that they have
[00:23:17] nowhere else to go. And so JustSpeak also wants to
[00:23:21] acknowledge the impact that this bill would have on young people.
[00:23:24] The bill extends these powers,
[00:23:26] orders to be imposed on people as young as 14.
[00:23:30] Manaaki Rangatahi: Responding to youth homelessness in Aotearoa New Zealand, a
[00:23:34] recent published Maori-led homelessness research paper, speaks about
[00:23:38] rangatahi as taonga, deserving protection, dignity, connection,
[00:23:41] and care. And we think that principle is important because it extends
[00:23:45] beyond rangatahi, because people experiencing homelessness are not
[00:23:49] problems that need to be managed. They're people with mana, with dignity,
[00:23:54] and they're members of our community.

[00:23:59] again, from a practical perspective, we note that, and I think the submission has
[00:24:02] been made to this committee earlier in this week, is that the

[00:24:14] So issues of disorderly conduct, violence, intimidation,
[00:24:18] blocking public ways
[00:24:21] are already addressed within the Summary Offences Act.

[00:24:46] to include
[00:24:47] homeless, well, symptoms of homelessness.

[03:27:53] the opinions expressed in this submission are our own.
[03:27:56] We are opposed to the Move on Orders Bill for the following reasons.
[03:28:01] I will be addressing two issues. First, why we submit the move on
[03:28:05] orders are inconsistent with fundamental rights and Te Tiriti
[03:28:09] obligations. Secondly, why we submit they are likely to increase
[03:28:13] incarceration while placing New Zealand at odds with its international
[03:28:17] obligations.
[03:28:18] Turning first to rights, our concern is that this bill
[03:28:22] gives police broad discretion to issue a move on order where they
[03:28:26] suspect someone has behaved in a disorderly or disruptive
[03:28:29] manner. Those concepts are broad, subjective, and open
[03:28:33] to inconsistent application. We submit that where
[03:28:37] Parliament grants powers that interfere with fundamental rights, they must be
[03:28:41] clearly defined, proportionate, and demonstrably justified.
[03:28:45] These concerns are further reinforced when viewed through the lens of Te
[03:28:48] Tiriti o Waitangi.
[03:28:50] Maori continue to experience disproportionately high rates of homelessness and
[03:28:54] severe housing deprivation. We are particularly concerned about
[03:28:58] the impact on rangatahi, as the bill applies to young people aged 14
[03:29:02] or over.
[03:29:04] For a young person sleeping rough, a move on order does not resolve the
[03:29:07] circumstances that brought them there.
[03:29:09] Instead, it may require them to leave an area where they feel relatively safe
[03:29:13] or risk breaching the order and entering the criminal justice system.
[03:29:17] We submit that this disproportionate impact engages the Crown's obligations
[03:29:20] of partnership and active protection under Te Tiriti.
[03:29:24] And turning briefly to our second point, our written submission explains why
[03:29:28] we are concerned that the bill will increase incarceration.
[03:29:32] We would emphasize that this is not because the bill responds to conduct
[03:29:35] that police currently cannot address.
[03:29:39] Existing provisions under the Summary Offences Act already
[03:29:43] provide police with powers to respond to genuinely threatening or disorderly
[03:29:47] behavior. Instead, the bill creates a new offense for breaching a move on
[03:29:51] order, carrying the possibility of imprisonment.
[03:29:54] We therefore question whether this additional offense is either necessary or
[03:29:57] proportionate. Finally, we submit that these concerns
[03:30:01] extend beyond domestic law. New Zealand has
[03:30:04] longstanding commitments under international human rights
[03:30:08] instruments, including the International Covenant on Civil and
[03:30:12] Political Rights. Those commitments reinforce the importance of protecting
[03:30:16] freedom of movement, expression, and peaceful assembly while ensuring imprisonment
[03:30:20] is used consistently with human dignity and only where justified.
[03:30:24] Ultimately, we submit that the bill expands the reach of the criminal law without
[03:30:27] addressing the circumstances that give rise to the conduct it seeks to regulate.
[03:30:31] For those reasons, we respectfully ask the committee to recommend that the bill not
[03:30:35] proceed. Ngā mihi nui. I will now pass on to Gemma.
[03:30:40] Kia ora.

[03:30:57] is a poor approach.
[03:30:59] Scotland's like us. They have overcrowded prisons with staff stretched to their
[03:31:03] limits, meaning that rehabilitative opportunities are limited and remand numbers
[03:31:07] are on the rise. Considering this, their Justice Directorate recently found,
[03:31:11] among other things, that having a high prison population does not
[03:31:15] equate to a safer society, and that reconviction rates are actually higher for
[03:31:19] those imprisoned for short sentences.
[03:31:21] And finally, that general overcrowding reduces the effectiveness of rehabilitative
[03:31:25] programs for people convicted of more serious offenses.
[03:31:29] So, the point to draw from that is that if, as per kind order statistics,
[03:31:33] 6,000 Kiwis are without accommodation each night, then it's actually for the good
[03:31:37] of all of us that those people first and foremost are provided a home and

[05:01:43] harmful, threatening, or distressing.
[05:01:46] These concerns deserve an effective response.
[05:01:49] However, public safety is not strengthened by moving people
[05:01:53] experiencing hardship, and many of who are not engaging in
[05:01:57] social behavior out of sight. Sorry, antisocial behavior.
[05:02:02] The bill
[05:02:05] creates wide powers to exclude people from public places.
[05:02:09] That may shift where hardship is seen, but it will not reduce harm,
[05:02:13] prevent homelessness, or create safer communities.
[05:02:17] I know from my engagements around this bill that in speaking to two primary
[05:02:21] concerns today, I will be reiterating points made by many
[05:02:24] other submitters. The two areas I wish to briefly highlight
[05:02:28] today are that the bill is a poor solution to a complex
[05:02:32] social problem, and that it is unnecessary and unworkable.
[05:02:36] Before doing so, I wish to briefly remind the committee of the human rights
[05:02:40] engaged by the bill as set out in our written submission.
[05:02:44] These include the right to adequate housing and the right to the
[05:02:48] highest attainable standard of physical and mental health.
[05:02:52] Move on orders also engage several fundamental civil and political
[05:02:56] rights, including freedom of movement, expression, peaceful
[05:03:00] assembly and association, and the right to liberty and security
[05:03:04] of the person. These rights are protected in the New Zealand Bill of
[05:03:08] Rights Act 1990 and the International Covenant on
[05:03:12] Civil and Political Rights, to which New Zealand is a
[05:03:15] party. New Zealand's implementation of the
[05:03:19] ICCPR is scheduled for a
[05:03:23] review by the United Nations Human Rights Committee in
[05:03:27] 2027. Should the bill be enacted, it is
[05:03:30] likely to be of significant interest to the committee.
[05:03:34] And I draw this to the committee's attention because New Zealand's strong
[05:03:38] record in upholding civil and political rights
[05:03:41] underpins its international reputation,
[05:03:45] including its diplomatic relationships, trade interests, and overall
[05:03:49] effectiveness on the global stage.
[05:03:53] First, the bill is a poor solution to a complex legal
[05:03:56] problem. The bill is ineffective and a
[05:04:00] counterproductive response to the problems it seeks to address.
[05:04:04] I note that the commission is not alone in this view.
[05:04:06] Many social service providers, religious leaders, businesses,
[05:04:10] Retail New Zealand, and the Police Association, and the Ministries of
[05:04:14] Justice and Housing have either opposed or raised
[05:04:18] significant concerns about using move on powers to
[05:04:22] respond to homelessness, poverty, and low-level public disorder.
[05:04:27] The RIS reaches a similar conclusion.
[05:04:30] It states that formal law enforcement responses are neither
[05:04:33] appropriate nor effective responses for public
[05:04:37] disorder that falls below the criminal threshold, and that
[05:04:40] move on orders are not necessary because other responses may be more
[05:04:44] appropriate. The bill is likely to have discriminatory
[05:04:48] intersectional impacts because the proposed grounds for issuing a move
[05:04:52] on order include begging, rough sleeping, and conduct
[05:04:56] said to indicate an intent to occupy
[05:05:00] a public place are closely associated with issues which
[05:05:04] disproportionately affect certain groups.
[05:05:07] These issues include poverty, homelessness, unmet disability support
[05:05:11] needs, mental distress, addiction, family violence,
[05:05:15] and housing insecurity. They're caused by barriers to inadequate
[05:05:19] housing, healthcare, income support, safety, and
[05:05:23] public participation. Barriers such as discrimination,
[05:05:27] which the Special Rapporteur on the right to adequate housing has
[05:05:31] identified as both a cause and a consequence of
[05:05:34] homelessness.
[05:05:36] The bill may have the compounding effect of making already marginalized
[05:05:40] people less visible to outreach workers and community
[05:05:44] support services, disrupting informal safety networks
[05:05:48] and pushing people into more isolated or unsafe
[05:05:52] locations. There are better solutions to address the concerns
[05:05:55] this bill attempts to respond to. A human rights based
[05:05:59] approach requires the state to identify and address the
[05:06:02] structural drivers and social determinants of issues
[05:06:06] like homelessness and disorderly conduct.
[05:06:11] This requires accessible, culturally safe, disability
[05:06:14] responsive, gender affirming, trauma informed, and
[05:06:18] community-led houses and support services.
[05:06:22] There is no provision in the bill to facilitate these.
[05:06:25] The focus on reform should be on evidence-based
[05:06:29] solutions that address the root causes of homelessness.
[05:06:33] Effective approaches already exist, including properly
[05:06:36] resourcing Housing First, investing in kaupapa Maori housing,
[05:06:40] and significantly
[05:06:42] expanding public
[05:06:45] housing supply. These must be accompanied by sustained
[05:06:49] investment in outreach and integrated social services to ensure people can
[05:06:53] access the support they need to secure
[05:06:57] and sustain housing.
[05:06:59] Secondly, the bill is unnecessary and unworkable.
[05:07:03] The bill creates new powers in an area where the law already
[05:07:07] provides police and local authorities with a range of tools to
[05:07:10] respond to genuinely harmful or disorderly
[05:07:13] conduct. The commission is therefore concerned that the bill
[05:07:17] duplicates existing offenses and powers rather than filling a
[05:07:21] clear legal gap.
[05:07:23] Where conduct is genuinely threatening, harmful, or
[05:07:27] criminal, it can already be addressed.
[05:07:30] The proposed response is disproportionate.
[05:07:33] The Ministry of Justice's offense and penalty vetting
[05:07:37] process found that the proposed offense for breaching a
[05:07:41] move on order is disproportionate to the harm caused and would
[05:07:44] create inconsistencies in the law.
[05:07:48] Sentences of imprisonment should be reserved for serious offending given the
[05:07:52] significant infringement on human rights that imprisonment has.
[05:07:56] There is a significant risk of inconsistency and arbitrary
[05:08:00] application and enforcement. The bill gives police
[05:08:03] significant discretion to issue move on
[05:08:07] orders on the basis of broad and potentially subjective
[05:08:11] concepts, including disorderly or disruptive
[05:08:14] behavior, begging, rough sleeping, and conduct said to
[05:08:18] indicate an intent to occupy a public place.
[05:08:23] In policing, discretion is a double-edged sword.
[05:08:26] It can allow police to make appropriate decisions about
[05:08:30] whether or not the conduct they're dealing with merits a punitive
[05:08:33] response. However, it may also mean orders are issued to
[05:08:37] people simply because they are visibly poor, homeless, distressed,
[05:08:41] disabled, or present in public space in
[05:08:45] ways that others consider uncomfortable or undesirable.
[05:08:50] Whilst discretion will allow police to make judgment calls, it also
[05:08:54] introduces a real risk of inconsistent or discriminatory
[05:08:57] application if decisions are made based on appearance,
[05:09:01] location, perceived social status, complaints from particular
[05:09:05] members of the public, or stereotypes around homelessness
[05:09:09] and public disorder.
[05:09:11] The bill also creates difficult legal distinctions and
[05:09:15] likely unintended consequences.
[05:09:18] For example, the bill exempts people whose primary
[05:09:22] purpose is demonstrating support for opposition
[05:09:25] to or publicizing a point of view, cause, or
[05:09:28] campaign.
[05:09:30] This may mean that a person protesting homelessness or poverty while sleeping rough
[05:09:34] or seeking donations is protected from a move on order,
[05:09:38] while another person in the same circumstances but without an
[05:09:42] expressly political message is not.
[05:09:45] For these reasons, the
[05:09:47] commission recommends that the bill should not proceed.
[05:09:50] More effective and rights-consistent solutions would focus on preventing
[05:09:54] and ending homelessness through stable, accessible housing,
[05:09:58] accessible health and addiction services, outreach, whanau-centered
[05:10:02] support, and stronger social protections.
[05:10:05] Addressing the underlying root causes of poverty would directly and
[05:10:09] significantly improve the safety and wellbeing of communities
[05:10:13] in an enduring way. If the bill does proceed, the commission
[05:10:17] recommends in line with option two proposed in the RIS
[05:10:21] that begging, rough sleeping, and any intent to
[05:10:25] occupy grounds be removed. That move on orders
[05:10:29] not apply to anyone under 18.
[05:10:32] That there is a requirement for the issuing of orders to only be
[05:10:36] done when strictly necessary and proportionate to the circumstances,
[05:10:41] and that there is a requirement for connection to referral
[05:10:44] pathways for housing, health, and social support
[05:10:48] responses when an order is issued Thank you again for the
